Asymptotic models for time-domain scattering by small particles of arbitrary shapes
Maryna Kachanovska, Adrian Savchuk

Abstract
In this work, we investigate time-dependent wave scattering by multiple small particles of arbitrary shape. To approximate the solution of the associated boundary-value problem, we derive an asymptotic model that is valid in the limit as the particle size tends to zero. Our method relies on a boundary integral formulation, semi-discretized in space using a Galerkin approach with appropriately chosen basis functions, s.t. convergence is achieved as the particle size vanishes rather than by increasing the number of basis functions. Since the computation of the Galerkin matrix involves double integration over particles, the method can become computationally demanding when the number of obstacles is large. To address this, we also derive a simplified model and consider the Born approximation to improve computational efficiency. For the high-order models, we provide an error analysis, supported and validated by numerical experiments.



1
Introduction

In this work, we consider the problem of the three-dimensional time-dependent scattering by many particles of arbitrary shape, stated as a boundary-value problem for the wave equation. In the first place, we are interested in the asymptotic regime when the diameter of the particles tends to zero, while their number and the distance between particles remains fixed.
Our goal is to find an alternative, more computationally advantageous model for this problem.

Such models have already appeared in the literature, cf. in particular,
[
29
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
20
]
. Since the two latter papers propose an asymptotic model based on the matching asymptotic expansion, however, for a single particle only, we concentrate our discussion on the model suggested in
[
29
]
, which handles multiple particle case. Its stated convergence rate
1
1

1

We would like to remark that the final goal of the work
[
29
]
is to consider a different, more complicated asymptotic regime, for which this accuracy appears to be sufficient since it is dominated by other sources of errors.
is
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, with
\varepsilon
being a characteristic size of the particles. However, for some geometric configurations, when, loosely speaking,
\varepsilon
is not ’small enough’, this model can suffer of spurious resonances, which lead to exponentially growing solutions in the time domain. See for a discussion of an analogous issue in two dimensions and
[
27
]
for an illustration of the existing issue in
[
29
]
.

In the present manuscript we suggest a model that has a higher convergence rate
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, and avoids geometry restrictions and source regularity requirements of
[
29
]
. To do so, we adopt the approach of
[
19
]
to construct an asymptotic model. We reformulate it using a single-layer boundary integral equation and semi-discretize it in space with the help of a suitable ’asymptotic’ Galerkin method, so that the convergence of the approximate solution is achieved by decreasing the asymptotic parameter rather than by increasing the number of basis functions.
This solution is a priori stable in the time domain due to the coercivity properties of the underlying operator.

The main difficulty of this method lies in the choice of appropriate basis functions for the asymptotic Galerkin space. In particular, it was shown in
[
19
]
that for the case of circular particles, using constant basis functions yields quadratic-order convergence for the scattered field. However, this choice is insufficient to ensure convergence in the general case of arbitrarily shaped obstacles.
An alternative idea, inspired by the works of
[
12
,
29
]
, is to use equilibrium densities, i.e., solutions of the single-layer boundary integral equation for the Laplacian with a constant right-hand side, as basis functions. We prove that this choice leads to the third-order convergence of the scattered field in 3D, and confirm this result by the numerical experiments.

Unlike the case of circles or spheres, where all entries of the Galerkin matrix can be computed explicitly, see
[
19
]
, the new method requires double integration, which is computationally expensive, especially as the number of particles increases.
To address this issue, we also derive a simplified model that preserves the stability and convergence properties of the GFL model. We compare the GFL model with the simplified model and the Born approximation, and provide a theoretical error analysis, further supported by numerical experiments.

This paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the problem setting, notation, and definitions, as well as the asymptotic models considered in this paper, together with the main result on their stability and convergence. Section
3
presents the derivation of the GFL model along with its error analysis.
A simplified model and the Born approximation are studied in Section
6
. Section
7
provides numerical validation of the theoretical results, along with a comparison of the asymptotic models.

2
Problem setting and main result

2.1
Problem setting

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and for each
k=1,\dots,N
, let
\Omega_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a Lipschitz domain with the boundary
\Gamma_{k}:=\partial\Omega_{k}
. Let
\Omega_{k}
be pairwise disjoint, i.e. it holds that
\overline{\Omega_{k}}\cap\overline{\Omega_{\ell}}=\emptyset
, for all
k\neq\ell
. We denote by
\boldsymbol{c}_{k}\in\Omega_{k}
a fixed point in
\Omega_{k}
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
be a scaling parameter. The rescaled domains
\Omega_{k}^{\varepsilon}
are defined by

\displaystyle\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\boldsymbol{x}=\varepsilon(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})+\boldsymbol{c}_{k},\,\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Omega_{k}\}.

(2.1)

By this definition, the rescaled domain is given by
\Omega^{\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{N}\Omega_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, with boundary
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{N}\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. For brevity, we use the notations
\Omega
and
\Gamma
instead of
\Omega^{1}
and
\Gamma^{1}
. We assume additionally that

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}\subseteq B_{k},\quad B_{k}:=B(\boldsymbol{c}_{k},r_{k}),\text{ so that }\Omega\subseteq B:=\cup_{k=1}^{N}B_{k},

(2.2)

where
B(\boldsymbol{c}_{k},r_{k})
denotes a ball centered at
\boldsymbol{c}_{k}
of radius
r_{k}>0
. Evidently,
r_{k}\geq\operatorname{diam}\Omega_{k}/2
. It is straightforward to see that
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{k}\subseteq B^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=B(\boldsymbol{c}_{k},r^{\varepsilon}_{k})
, where
r^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=\varepsilon r_{k}
.
Additionally, let us assume that for all
k\neq\ell
,
\overline{B_{k}}\cap\overline{B_{\ell}}=\emptyset
. This implies that
\overline{B_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\cap\overline{B_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}=\emptyset
for all
0<\varepsilon<1
.
The minimal distance between balls
B_{k}^{\varepsilon}
is denoted by

\displaystyle d_{*}^{\varepsilon}:=\min_{k\neq{\ell}}\operatorname{dist}(B_{k}^{\varepsilon},B_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}).

(2.3)

Remark that for all
0<\varepsilon<1
,
d_{*}^{\varepsilon}>0
and
d_{*}^{\varepsilon}\leq\min_{k\neq\ell}\text{dist}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k},\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
.

Figure 1
:
An example of domains
\Omega_{k}
and
\Omega_{\ell}
, where
d_{k\ell}:=\operatorname{dist}(B_{k},B_{\ell})
.

We assume that the field
u^{\operatorname{inc}}:\,\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies the free space wave equation:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial^{2}_{t}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)-\Delta u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=0,&\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0},\\
u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},0)=u_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\partial_{t}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},0)=u_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}),&\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{array}

with real-valued
u_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\,u_{1}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
being compactly supported in
B^{c}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}
.
We seek the solution to the sound-soft scattering problem. Namely, we look for the total field
u^{\varepsilon,\operatorname{tot}}
in the form
u^{\varepsilon}+u^{\operatorname{inc}}
, where
u^{\varepsilon}
is the scattered field that satisfies the wave equation in the exterior of the domain
\Omega^{\varepsilon}
, namely
\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{\Omega^{\varepsilon}},

with the inhomogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial^{2}_{t}u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)-\Delta u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=0,&\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0},\\
\gamma_{0}u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=g^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t):=-\gamma_{0}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t),&\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0},\\
u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},0)=0,\quad\partial_{t}u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},0)=0,&\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}.\\
\end{array}

Remark that, because of the finite speed of the wave propagation and the support of the source term,
\left.u^{\operatorname{inc}}(t)\right|_{B}=0
for all
t\in[0,\delta)
, with
\delta>0
independent of
\varepsilon
.

It is well-known that solving the above problem numerically using classical methods, such as Finite Element Methods (FEM) combined with explicit time-stepping schemes, requires meshing finely in the vicinity of the small obstacles
\Omega^{\varepsilon}
, which can make efficient computations prohibitively expensive, see
[
7
]
.

On the other hand, a frequency-domain argument shows that
\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(K))}
is
O(\varepsilon)
for any compact subset
K
of
\Omega^{c}
. Nonetheless, in many practical applications (cf. e.g.
[
9
,
11
,
25
]
), simply approximating
u^{\varepsilon}
by zero appears to be insufficient. Therefore, our goal is to construct an approximate solution
u^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{app}}
of the scattered filed
u^{\varepsilon}
by deriving a higher-order asymptotic model valid in the regime as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

2.2
Preliminary notation and definitions

2.2.1
Functions on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}

First of all, where applicable, by bold symbols (e.g.
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
) we will denote quantities defined on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
, and with the index
k
(e.g.
\varphi_{k}
) and a regular font we will mark their restrictions to
\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
; in other words,
\varphi_{k}:=\left.\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right|_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}
.

H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
spaces

We define the space
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
as a space of functions

\displaystyle H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):=\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):\,\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}<+\infty\},

with
\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}:=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\|\varphi_{k}\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}
.
Each of the norms
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
is defined as a (usual) Sobolev-Slobodeckij norm:
\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}:=\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}+|\psi|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}
, with

\displaystyle|\psi|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}^{2}=\iint_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\times\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{|\psi(\boldsymbol{x})-\psi(\boldsymbol{y})|^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{3}}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(2.9)

By
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, we denote the dual space to
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
with the following norm:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}|}{\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}},\quad\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),

(2.10)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}
, refers to the duality pairing between
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
and
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
spaces, and for
\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, we have that

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{x})}{\boldsymbol{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x})}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{1/2,-1/2}.

Often we will write instead
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. A standard argument shows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|\psi_{k}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.\end{split}

(2.11)

2.2.2
Fourier-Laplace transform

Let
X
be a separable Hilbert space and
f:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\to X
be a tempered
X
-valued distribution;
f
is a causal if
\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq[0,+\infty)
.
This class of distributions is denoted by
\operatorname{CT}(X)
(see
[
28
, Section 2.1]
).
For sufficiently regular
f\in\operatorname{CT}(X)
, the Fourier-Laplace transform is given by

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\omega)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{i\omega t}f(t)\,dt,\quad\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\,\operatorname{Im}z>0\},

(2.12)

via the Bochner integral. We will need additionally a class of vector-valued distributions
\operatorname{TD}(X)
which are defined as follows

\displaystyle\operatorname{TD}(X):=\{\varphi\in\operatorname{CT}(X):~\exists m\geq 0,~\text{and}~F\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{pol}(\mathbb{R};X),~\text{s.t.}~\varphi=\left(\frac{d}{dt}\right)^{m}F\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{0}_{pol}(\mathbb{R};X)=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R};X):f=0~\text{on}~(-\infty,0)

\displaystyle\hskip 140.0pt\text{and}~\exists p\geq 0:~\|(1+t)^{-p}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{>0};X)}<\infty\},

where
d/dt
is the distributional derivative. In other words,
\operatorname{TD}(X)
are distributional derivatives of continuous causal functions of polynomial growth.
The distributions
\operatorname{TD}(X)
satisfy the following property (cf. Proposition 3.1.2 and 3.1.3 of
[
28
]
):

\displaystyle\varphi\in\operatorname{TD}(X)\iff

\displaystyle\omega\mapsto\hat{\varphi}(\omega)\text{ is $X$-analytic in }\mathbb{C}^{+}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\|\hat{\varphi}(\omega)\|_{X}\leq C(1+|\omega|)^{p}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-k}),~\text{for some}~p,k\geq 0.

In what follows, we will also need a larger space
[
4
]

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{+}(X):=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X),\,\text{ for which there exists }\sigma_{0}\geq 0,\text{ s.t. }\mathrm{e}^{-\sigma_{0}t}\varphi\in\operatorname{CT}(X)\},

which, roughly speaking, allows for the exponential growth of distributions, and for which we can define the Fourier-Laplace transform in the usual manner. The Fourier-Laplace transforms of such distributions are characterized by the following property, cf.
[
4
]
or
[
14
, Chapter 16]

\displaystyle\varphi\in{L}^{\prime}_{+}(X)\iff

\displaystyle\exists\,\sigma\geq 0,\text{s.t. }\omega\mapsto\hat{\varphi}(\omega)\text{ is $X$-analytic in }\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\sigma}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Im}z>\sigma\}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\|\hat{\varphi}(\omega)\|_{X}\leq C(1+|\omega|)^{p},\quad\text{for some}\quad p\geq 0.

Let us recall as well that the Laplace transform is injective on
L^{\prime}_{+}(X)
.

While all the quantities in this paper are defined for
t\geq 0
, in order to apply the above formalism to them, we extend them by
0
to
(-\infty,0)
.

Convolutions.
In what follows, we will use the following notation for convolutions. Recall that given two causal tempered distributions
\operatorname{CT}(\mathbb{R})
,
\varphi,\psi
, their convolution is denoted by
\varphi*\psi
. In particular, for
\varphi,\psi
sufficiently regular, we have

\displaystyle(\varphi*\psi)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(t-\tau)\psi(\tau)d\tau.

We will also use the notation
\varphi(t)*\psi(t)
, where it facilitates the understanding.

2.2.3
Auxiliary notaiton

Occasionally, we will write
a\lesssim b
to indicate that there exists a constant
C>0
that depends on the shapes of the particles
\Omega_{j}
only (i.e. independent of
\varepsilon,N,\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*}
,
\omega
in the Fourier-Laplace transform), s.t.
a\leq Cb
.

2.3
Principal results of the paper

In the present paper we suggest three asymptotic models. The first one will serve as a basis, for which we will derive all the results. As we see later, an intuition for it comes from the numerical analysis, see
[
19
]
, and the convergence analysis is inspired by similar ideas. The second model is much simpler; it will be obtained as a perturbation of the first model. Finally, the third model is a Born-style approximation, which appears to have a rather good convergence order in our setting (compared to two dimensions, cf.
[
10
]
, where the existing estimates indicate that it converges very slowly). To state these models, let us introduce, for
t,r\geq 0
, the kernel of the retarded potential operator in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(r,t)=\frac{\delta(t-r)}{4\pi r}.

An asymptotic model 1

The approximate scattered field takes the following form:

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\mathcal{G}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|,t)*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(t)\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})\,d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}

(2.13)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})\,d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0},

(2.14)

where the time-dependent real-valued functions
\{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(\cdot)\}_{k=1}^{N}
solve the following linear convolutional in time system:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{k=1}^{N}(K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k})(t)=q_{G,\ell}^{\varepsilon}(t),\text{ where }\\
&(K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k})(t)=\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\int_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\\
&q^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell}(t)=-\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}},\end{split}

(2.15)

and
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
satisfies the following problem:
\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\,d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=1\text{ on }\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. This model is derived in Section
3
.

Remark 1
.

The quantities functions
{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}
depend on
\varepsilon
trivially, see (
4.20
). This means that in practice the equilibrium densities
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
can be computed once for
\varepsilon=1
and reused for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

The numerical implementation of the above model relies as well on computing surface integrals
\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\times\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
in (
2.15
). This can be computationally quite heavy, that is why in what follows we propose a model with similar properties, which avoids evaluation of these surface integrals.

An asymptotic model 2

Let
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
be like in (
2.15
).
Set

\displaystyle c_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(2.16)

With these definitions, the approximated scattered field takes the following form (where the index ’
s
’ stands for simplified):

\displaystyle{u}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\frac{{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|}\left(c_{k}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{3}}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\partial_{t}{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon},

where the time-dependent real-valued functions
\{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}(\cdot)\}_{k=1}^{N}
solve the following linear convolutional in time system:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{k=1}^{N}(K^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k})(t)={q}_{s}^{\varepsilon,\ell}(t),\text{ with }\\
&q^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell}(t)=-u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)c_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},\\
&K^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k})(t),\end{split}

(2.17)

and the matrix
\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}(\omega)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}4\pi i\omega(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{2}j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})h^{(1)}_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}),&k=\ell,\\
4\pi i\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})h^{(1)}_{0}(\omega|\boldsymbol{c}_{k}-\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}|),&k\neq\ell.\end{array}\right.

with
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=c^{\varepsilon}_{k}/4\pi
, and
j_{0}
,
h^{(1)}_{0}
being the spherical Bessel and Hankel functions of the first kind
[
1
, Chapter 10.47]
. This model will be derived in Section
6.2
.

Remark 2
.

Numerical realization of this model with the help of convolution quadrature methods
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, compared to (
2.15
), relies solely on evaluation of
c_{k}^{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
.

An asymptotic model 3 (Born approximation)

Let
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
be like in (
2.15
) and
c_{k}^{\varepsilon}
as in (
2.16
). With these definitions, the approximated scattered field is given by:

\displaystyle{u}^{\varepsilon}_{B,\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\frac{u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{k},t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|}c_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

(2.18)

Remark 3
.

Compared to the previous models, the above model is computationally most efficient, and relies solely on knowing the capacitances
c_{k}^{\varepsilon}
.

Well-posedness, stability and the error estimates

Let us introduce the following definitions. The first definition concerns the geometry.

Definition 2.1
.

A family
(\Omega^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
is admissible if
d^{\varepsilon}_{*}>0
for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
.

It is straightforward to see that
\varepsilon\mapsto d^{\varepsilon}_{*}
is increasing in
\varepsilon>0
, thus
(\Omega^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
is admissible if and only if
d^{1}_{*}>0
.

Next, we need to define a notion of well-posedness for those models that are based on the computation of auxiliary quantities
t\mapsto\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\varepsilon}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, namely (
2.15
) and (
2.17
).

Definition 2.2
.

We will call an asymptotic model well-posed if, for any admissible geometry, the following holds true.

There exist
\ell_{T},\ell_{S},\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(where S and T stand for ”space” and ”time”, resp.), s.t., provided a solution to (
2.1
)
u^{\operatorname{inc}}\in\operatorname{TD}(H^{\ell_{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap C^{\ell_{T}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};H^{\ell_{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, the asymptotic model admits a unique solution
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , and, moreover,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

To define a stable and convergent models, we will restrict our attention to the behaviour of the scattered field on the compact sets outside
2

2

This is done for technical reasons, since some of our convergence estimates rely on the fact that
K
does not intersect any of convex hulls of
\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{k}
.
of
B=\cup_{k=1}^{N}B_{k}
, cf. (
2.2
).

Definition 2.3
.

We will call an asymptotic model which yields an approximate solution
u^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
uniformly stable if there exist constants
m,\ell_{S},\ell_{T}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, s.t. the following holds true. For any admissible geometric configuration
\Omega^{\varepsilon}
, all compacts
K
with
K\cap\overline{B}=\emptyset
, there exists
C_{\operatorname{geom},K}:=C_{\operatorname{geom}}(d_{*}^{\varepsilon},\Omega,K)\leq C_{\operatorname{geom}}(d^{1}_{*},\Omega,K)<+\infty
, s.t. the following bound holds true uniformly for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in K}|u^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)|\leq(1+t)^{m}C_{\operatorname{geom},K}\|u^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{\ell_{T}}(0,t;H^{\ell_{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))}.

We will call such a model convergent of order
p
, if, additionally, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in K}|u^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)-u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)|\leq\varepsilon^{p+1}(1+t)^{m}C_{\operatorname{geom},K}\|u^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{\ell_{T}}(0,t;H^{\ell_{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))},.

for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
and
t\geq 0
. From the frequency-domain reasoning, it follows that for a single-particle case
N=1
, for a fixed
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{c}
, the scattered field behaves as
u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=O(\varepsilon)
(with a hidden constant that depends on
t
), thus
p
in the above definition refers to a relative error. E.g., with this convention, the model of
[
29
]
under our assumptions is convergent of order
p=1
.

Remark 4
.

At a first sight, the above notion of stability is quite weak since it does not reflect the decay of scattered waves in compact sets of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, allowing for a polynomial growth in time. However, ensuring that asymptotic models do not exhibit even exponential growth is not fully trivial, cf.
[
19
]
and geometrical conditions in
[
29
]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

The models (
2.15
), (
2.17
) are well-posed; together with the model (
2.18
), they are uniformly stable.

The models (
2.15
) and (
2.17
) are convergent of order
p=2
.

The model (
2.18
) is convergent of order
p=1
.

Proof.

The statement about the model (
2.15
) is detailed and proven in Theorems
5.1
,
5.2
. The result for the model (
2.17
) is proven in Section
6.2
. The results for the Born model (
2.18
) can be deduced from the estimates of Section
6.2
, see Appendix
E
for details.
∎

The statement about the convergence of the model (
2.18
) for spherical particles can be found in
[
7
]
, but, up to our knowledge, no proof exists for scattering by multiple particles. It is quite interesting to compare this convergence statement with the known results in 2D, where the Born model in the frequency domain seems to yield a slow convergence (approximating the field with
O(\log^{-2}\varepsilon)
absolute accuracy), see
[
10
]
.

In
[
12
]
, it was remarked that the first order model can be constructed by knowing particle capacitances only; in other words, the field scattered by spheres and by domains of the same capacitances would agree up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
-term. The model (
2.17
) yields the approximation of the scattered field up
O(\varepsilon^{3})
-term, and requires knowing capacitances and the first moments
\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
only. It is not difficult to see that in the case when all particles have a reflectional symmetry with respect to coordinate axes centered in
\boldsymbol{c}_{k}
, the first moments
\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{0}
, and in this case approximating particles by spheres of equivalent capacitances yields the model of order
p=2

3
3

3

The authors are grateful to David Hewett (UCL, UK) for this question.
.

Remark 5
.

The models (
2.15
) and (
2.18
) can be shown to be well-posed and stable for any geometric configuration with
\operatorname{dist}(\Omega_{j}^{\varepsilon},\Omega_{k}^{\varepsilon})>0
rather than
\operatorname{dist}(B^{\varepsilon}_{j},B^{\varepsilon}_{k})>0
; however, such a stability proof for (
2.15
) is more technical, and hence we omit it here.

3
Derivation of the asymptotic model (
2.15
)

To derive the asymptotic model (
2.15
), we seek the scattered field
u^{\varepsilon}
as the convolution of the retarded potential with a density
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}:\,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\mathcal{G}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|,t)*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y},t)d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},

(3.1)

for
(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For the moment we will not make precise the function spaces, referring the interested reader to the monographs
[
28
,
6
]
, or the seminal work on well-posedness of TDBIE
[
4
]
, see as well
[
23
]
. In order to restore
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{k}
, we apply the trace operator to both sides of (
3.1
), and using the Dirichlet boundary conditions (
2.1
) and continuity of the single-layer potential across the boundary yields the new problem:

\displaystyle(S^{\varepsilon}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

(3.2)

Here, for sufficiently regular
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
¡

\displaystyle(S^{\varepsilon}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y},t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\equiv\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y},t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}.

The problem (
3.2
) can be shown to be well-posed in a suitable functional framework (Laplace transformable causal distributions), cf. the seminal work of Bamberger and Ha-Duong
[
4
]
or the monograph
[
28
]
for more details. In particular, we have

Theorem 3.1

(
[
4
]
,
[
28
]
)
.

For all
\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{TD}(H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
, there exists a unique solution to (
3.2
)
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{+}^{\prime}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
. Additionally, this solution lies in
\operatorname{TD}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
.

3.1
An abstract asymptotic Galerkin method

As suggested in
[
19
]
, we will derive an asymptotic model as a semi-discretization of the above problem in space by a suitable Galerkin method, with a set of basis functions given by
\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
. This method satisfies the following conditions:

•

\text{dim}\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=N_{B}
, where
N_{B}
is fixed for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
;

•

the basis functions in
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
behave in a trivial manner with respect to
\varepsilon
(the notion of ’trivial’ will be clear in Section
3.2
);

•

the convergence will be ensured by
\varepsilon\to 0
, instead of
N_{B}\to\infty
.

The main advantage of this approach lies in the fact that, due to coercivity properties of the single-layer boundary integral operators, such Galerkin semi-discretizations yield stable in the time domain models, see
[
5
]
.

As in the classical Galerkin semi-discretization in space, let us choose a finite-dimensional space
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\subseteq H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, which is defined as

\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\}_{k=1}^{N},

where the basis linear forms
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
are non-trivial on
\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
and vanish on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\setminus\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, i.e.
e^{\varepsilon}_{k,\ell}=0
for
\ell\neq k
.
We look for the approximate density

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(t)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R},

(3.3)

where
\{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(\cdot)\}_{k=1}^{N}
are unknown time-dependent causal functions.

Testing the boundary equation (
3.2
) with
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
and using (
3.3
), for
k=1,\ldots,N
and
t>0
, yields the following convolutional in time linear system

\displaystyle\langle(S^{\varepsilon,kk}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}e_{k,k}^{\varepsilon})(t),e_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}+\sum_{\ell\neq k}\langle(S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell}e_{\ell,\ell}^{\varepsilon})(t),e_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\cdot,t),e_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}},

(3.4)

where
S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}
denotes the restricted to
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}
single layer operator defined on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
, i.e., for causal and sufficiently regular
\varphi
,

\displaystyle(S^{\varepsilon,k{\ell}}*\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x},t):=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\frac{\varphi(\boldsymbol{y},t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Once the approximate densities are found, the field can be approximated by applying the single layer potential to (
3.3
):

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{app}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{\lambda_{G,k}^{\varepsilon}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}e_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

(3.5)

3.2
Choice of basis functions for
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}

In this section, we derive basis functions
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
. This choice is inspired by the ideas from
[
29
]
, namely by the proof of Theorem 2.5. For convenience of the reader, we present a very formal derivation of the basis functions here, referring to Theorem 2.5 of
[
29
]
for a rigorous statement.

The choice of basis functions for the single-particle case

We start by considering the single-particle case
N=1
with
\boldsymbol{c}_{1}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Provided sufficiently regular data, the exact causal density
\lambda^{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R};H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
satisfies the boundary integral equation

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y},t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=-u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t),\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

The key idea is to rewrite the above on a rescaled domain
\Gamma
, by defining
\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}:=\varepsilon^{-1}\boldsymbol{y}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},t-\varepsilon|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|)}{4\pi|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|}d\Gamma_{\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}}=-\varepsilon^{-1}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{x}},t),\quad(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Next, we formally approximate

\displaystyle\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},t-\varepsilon|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|)\approx\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},t),\quad u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{x}},t)\approx u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\mathbf{0},t),

which yields the new identity

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},t)}{4\pi|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|}d\Gamma_{\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}}\approx-\varepsilon^{-1}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\mathbf{0},t).

We recognize in the left-hand side of the above the single layer boundary integral operator for the Laplace equation, defined via
{S}_{0}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma
, while in the right-hand side a function constant on
\Gamma
, and thus

\displaystyle\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\approx-\varepsilon^{-1}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\mathbf{0},t){S}_{0}^{-1}1.

In other words, we will look for
\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\cdot,t)\in\operatorname{span}\{\sigma\}
, where
\sigma
is a unique
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)
solution to
{S}_{0}\sigma=1
. Now, let
\sigma^{\varepsilon}
denote
\sigma
defined on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
, i.e.
\sigma^{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\sigma^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=1,\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}.

The scaling argument yields

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\sigma^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{y}})}{4\pi|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|}d\Gamma_{\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}}=\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\sigma(\hat{\boldsymbol{y}})}{4\pi|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|}d\Gamma_{\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Gamma,

(3.6)

which implies that
\sigma^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})=\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})
, for
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Gamma
.
Thus, we chose the equilibrium density
\sigma^{\varepsilon}
as a basis function for
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, and using the definition of the asymptotic model (
3.4
) for the single-particle case yields that

\displaystyle\langle(S^{\varepsilon}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G}\sigma^{\varepsilon})(\cdot,t),\sigma^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}=\langle g^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t),\sigma^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Generalization to the many-particle case

In the general multiple-particle case, the Galerkin space takes the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N},\text{ where }

\displaystyle\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k,\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})=0\text{ for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell},\quad\text{ and solves }\quad\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k,k}(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=1,~\text{for}~\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.7)

For brevity, let us define
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\sigma_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The Galerkin space
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
is well-defined and its dimension equals
N
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
k=1,\ldots,N
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. By the well-known result
[
24
, Corollary 8.13]
, the equation in (
3.7
) admits a unique solution
\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
. By Theorem 3 in
[
13
]
(see also the references therein),
\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
, since
\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=1\in H^{1}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
.
∎

Plugging in this definition into (
3.4
) yields the final asymptotic model (
2.15
):

\displaystyle\langle(S^{\varepsilon,kk}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k})(t),\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}+\sum_{\ell\neq k}\langle(S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})(t),\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\langle g^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t),\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}},

(3.8)

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\lambda_{G,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

(3.9)

4
Stability and error analysis for the asymptotic model (
2.15
) in the frequency domain

Our stability and error analysis relies on the link between the Galerkin semi-discretization in space of (
3.2
) and the asymptotic model (
2.15
). It is carried out in the Fourier-Laplace domain and subsequently translated into the time domain. We begin by reformulating the problem (
3.2
) in the frequency domain using the Fourier-Laplace transform. The analysis is then divided into three parts. First, we derive an estimate for the density error in the Fourier-Laplace domain, expressed in terms of the norm of the data
\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon}
. Next, we use this estimate to obtain a corresponding bound for the scattered field via the retarded potential formulation. Finally, we translate the resulting bounds into the time domain by applying the Plancherel identity. We will demonstrate that, in order to obtain the optimal bound on the scattered field, it is necessary to decompose the density error into two components using an appropriate decomposition of the solution space
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
.

4.1
Frequency-domain counterparts of (
2.15
) and main results

The frequency-domain counterparts of (
3.1
) with the single layer boundary integral equation (
3.2
) in the Fourier-Laplace domain take the following form (we omit the dependence of unknowns and data on
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
): with
G_{\omega}(r)=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{4\pi r},\,r>0
,

\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})=(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\hat{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c},

(4.1)

\displaystyle S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\,\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})\,d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}.

(4.2)

The Fourier-Laplace transform of the approximate density (
3.3
) is defined via

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}(\boldsymbol{x},\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\hat{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(\omega)\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon},

(4.3)

and the final asymptotic model (
3.8
) becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}(\omega)\,\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon},\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}+\sum_{\ell\neq k}\hat{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell}(\omega)\,\langle S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}_{\omega}\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},\sigma_{k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon},\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}},

(4.4)

\displaystyle S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}_{\omega}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x}):=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}.

(4.5)

for
k=1,\dots,N
. The approximate field then satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{app}}(\boldsymbol{x},\omega)=(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\hat{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G,k}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}.

(4.6)

In our analysis, we will not work with the coefficients of the expansion
\hat{\lambda}_{G,k}^{\varepsilon}
, but rather with quantities
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
.

As a first step, we prove the following two results in the frequency domain, which, as we see later, by a standard argument, will yield Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
5.2
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Provided that
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}(\omega)\in H^{3}(B)
, the problem (
4.4
) admits a unique solution
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}(\omega)\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, which additionally satisfies:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}(\omega)\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})\|\hat{{u}}^{inc}(\omega)\|_{H^{3}(B)},

where the constant
C>0
depends on
\Gamma_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,N
(shape of the scatterers) only.

Proof.

Follows by combining Lemma
4.4
and Proposition
4.9
.
∎

The next result links the error of the scattered field and the norm of the data.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
K\subset B^{c}
be compact;
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Provided that
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}(\omega)\in H^{3}(B)
, the error between
\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
4.1
), and
\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{app}}
, defined in (
4.6
), is bounded by

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in K}|\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\omega)-\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{app}}(\boldsymbol{x},\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{3}C\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-9})(1+|\omega|)^{10}\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}(\omega)\|_{H^{3}(B)},

(4.7)

where
C=C_{K,\Omega}N^{5/2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-10}
, and
C_{K,\Omega}>0
depends on
K
and
\Gamma
.

Proof.

See Section
4.5
.
∎

Remark 6
.

The above result yields convergence in the frequency domain, and next we use the Plancherel identity to recover the time-domain bounds.

The rest of this section is dedicated to the proofs of these results. In order to avoid repeating some arguments, we chose to concentrate on the proof of convergence of the model, which relies on refined results about the behaviour of the single-layer boundary integral operator; a rougher version of these results implies the well-posedness. To derive (and even state) these results, we start by introducing the necessary tools.

4.2
Decompositions of spaces

4.2.1
Decomposition of spaces orthogonal with respect to the
L^{2}
-scalar product

Just like in
[
19
]
, the heart of the analysis will lie in the decomposition of the space
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
into two subspaces: space of obstacle-wise constant functions
\mathbb{S}_{0}
, see (
4.2.1
) (which is also a subspace of
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
) and linear functionals vanishing on
\mathbb{S}_{0}
, namely
H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
). However, such a decomposition is well-suited for the analysis on spherical particles (where, in particular,
\mathbb{S}_{0}
coincides with
\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
), while is less straightforward to use in the analysis for general obstacles. Thus, at some point, we will need to employ an auxiliary decomposition, analogous to the above, but which will allow us to treat independently
H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
and
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, see Section
4.2.2
.

We define the space of functions
\mathbb{S}_{0}
, constant on each of the obstacles:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{1}_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon},\\
0,&\text{ otherwise, }\end{array}\right.\text{ so that }\mathbb{S}_{0}:=\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{1}_{k}^{\varepsilon},\,k=1,\ldots,N\}.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{0}:\,H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
be an
L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
-orthogonal projector on
\mathbb{S}_{0}
;
\mathbb{P}_{\perp}:=\operatorname{Id}-\mathbb{P}_{0}
. With

\displaystyle H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):=\{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):\,(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=0\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{S}_{0}\},

it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ker}\mathbb{P}_{0}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}=H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),\qquad\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{P}_{0}=\operatorname{Ker}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}=\mathbb{S}_{0}.

(4.10)

The following result basically summarizes the above.

Proposition 4.1
.

The space
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
writes as a direct sum:
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})=\mathbb{S}_{0}\dotplus H^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),

with the decomposition orthogonal with respect to the
L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
- and
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
-scalar products. As a corollary,

\|\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{2}+\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{2}
.

We will need a counterpart of the above decomposition on the space
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. For this let us define adjoint projectors,
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*},\;\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}:\,H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, via

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}=\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}},\quad\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*},

(4.11)

and the dual counterpart of
H^{1/2}_{\perp}
:

H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):=\{\psi\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):\quad\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}=0,\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{S}_{0}\}.

From the definition (
4.11
), it follows that
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
for
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, in other words
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}
is an extension by density of
\mathbb{P}_{0}
to
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
.

By
[
24
, p. 23, Lemma 2.10]
, from the above definitions and (
4.10
), it is clear that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ker}\,\mathbb{P}^{*}_{\perp}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{P}^{*}_{0}=\mathbb{S}_{0},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{P}^{*}_{\perp}=\operatorname{Ker}\mathbb{P}^{*}_{0}=H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}).

As a corollary, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.2
.

H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})=\mathbb{S}_{0}\dotplus H^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),
where
\dotplus
is a direct sum.

4.2.2
Decomposition of spaces orthogonal with respect to the single-layer induced scalar product

As discussed in Section
4.2.1
, we will also need an alternative decomposition of the spaces
H^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. This decomposition, as we will see later (Section
4.3
), is well-adapted to the analysis of the Galerkin asymptotic method. In particular, let us introduce the following, localized on each obstacle, scalar product induced by the single layer boundary integral operator for the Laplace equation:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi})_{S_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\langle\psi_{k},\overline{S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{0}\varphi_{k}}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2,\,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}},\quad\boldsymbol{\psi},\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),\text{ where }

(4.12)

\displaystyle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{0}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k},\quad\varphi\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}).

Remark that the expression (
4.12
) indeed induces a scalar product in
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. This follows from the continuity and coercivity of
S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}
, see
[
30
, Theorem 6.2.2]
:

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\geq\langle\psi,\overline{S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}\psi}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\geq c_{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})}.

(4.13)

Let us define the orthogonal projection operator
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}:H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\to H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
on the
N
-dimensional space
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
: for all
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi}:=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma}
, where
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma}
is a unique element in
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\varphi})_{S_{0}}=0,\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G},

and
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
. We have the following explicit characterization of
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{1}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\rangle\left(c_{k}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

Here
c^{\varepsilon}_{k}
is the capacity:
c^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}>0
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. By definitions of
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon},\,\alpha^{\varepsilon}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
.
To find the coefficients
\alpha_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, we use the definition of
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon})_{S_{0}}=(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon})_{S_{0}},\quad\ell=1,\ldots,N.

(4.14)

Next, since
\sigma_{\ell,m}=0
for
m\neq\ell
, we have that:

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon})_{S_{0}}=\langle\psi_{\ell},S_{0}^{\varepsilon,\ell\ell}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}=\langle\psi_{\ell},1\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}.

Using the same identity with
\boldsymbol{\psi}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\sigma}
, and inserting the result into (
4.14
), we recover the coefficients
\alpha_{k}^{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\langle\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},1\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}=\langle\psi_{\ell},1\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}.

It remains to remark that by (
4.13
)

\displaystyle c_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}:=\langle\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},1\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}=\langle\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},S_{0}^{\varepsilon,\ell\ell}\sigma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}>0.

(4.15)

∎

From the above lemma it follows immediately that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{Ker}\,\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}=\operatorname{Im}\,\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}=H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),\qquad\text{Ker}\,\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}=\operatorname{Im}\,\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}.\end{split}

(4.16)

We will also need the following, immediate from (
4.16
), properties:

\displaystyle(a)\quad\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}=(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma})\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}=\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*},\qquad(b)\quad\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}.

(4.17)

Remark that the above projection operators have been defined on the dual space
H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. We will also need to define the adjoint projectors on the space
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
: for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}:=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2},

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}:=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}.

By
[
24
, p. 23, Lemma 2.10]
, from the above definition and (
4.16
) it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\operatorname{Im}\,\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}=\text{Ker}\,\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}=\mathbb{S}_{0},\qquad\operatorname{Im}\,\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}=\text{Ker}\,\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}=(\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G})^{a},\end{split}

(4.18)

where
(\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G})^{a}=\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}):\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle=0,\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\}
. The above implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}.

(4.19)

We also have an explicit characterization of the adjoint operators, see (
4.15
).

Lemma 4.2
.

For
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\langle\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle(c_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\boldsymbol{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k}.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\sigma}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. By definition of
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}
and by Lemma
4.1
:

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\sigma}}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle=\langle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rangle(c^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\langle\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle(c^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{-1}\boldsymbol{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\rangle.

∎

With the above lemma we see that for real-valued
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is real-valued, and therefore, for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2},\qquad\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\rangle_{-1/2,\,1/2}.

We will use these observations without referring to them explicitly.

4.2.3
Properties and relations between projectors

So far we have looked only at the algebraic properties of the subspaces and projectors. In this section we will establish relations between them and consider some topological (i.e. norm-related) properties. We start with an important fact about the space
H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
that follows from Corollary
A.2
.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}
, for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
.

The rest of the section will be dedicated to the interplay of the above property and the projectors introduced in Section
4.2.2
.
In what follows, it will be convenient to remark that from an elementary scaling argument, for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})=\varepsilon^{-1}\sigma_{k}^{1}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad\text{thus},\quad\|\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})}^{2}=\|\sigma_{k}^{1}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{k})}\quad\text{and}\quad c^{\varepsilon}_{k}=\varepsilon c_{k}^{1}.

(4.20)

It appears that the norms
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
and
L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
are equivalent on the space
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
with equivalence constants independent of
\varepsilon
.

Proposition 4.4
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

See Proposition
B.2
. ∎

Relations between projectors and bounds on their norms

The property below shows that the norms of
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
are uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon>0
.

Proposition 4.5
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}+\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}\leq C.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. By Lemma
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{2}_{-1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{|\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rangle|^{2}}{(c_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{2}}\|{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}C_{k}^{\varepsilon}\|{\psi}_{k}\|^{2}_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})},

where
C_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\|{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k,k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}(c_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{-2}
.
Using
\|{1}^{\varepsilon}_{k,k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
and (
4.20
), we conclude that
C_{k}^{\varepsilon}\lesssim 1
, and this yields the stated bound for
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
. The uniform bound on
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
follows from
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
, the triangle inequality and the bound on
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
.
∎

The following result allows us to extract information about
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi},\,\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
from
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
. Its proof can be found in Corollary
B.1
.

Proposition 4.6
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C\|\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C(\varepsilon^{1/2}\|\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}).

The next result enables us to work with the projector
\mathbb{P}_{\perp}
instead of
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

There exist
C,C^{\prime}>0
, s.t., for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C^{\prime}|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

The first bound follows from (
4.19
), namely
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}
, the identity
\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}=\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}
and Proposition
4.5
. The second bound is immediate from Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Similarly, we will also need a simplified stability bound for
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

Proof.

The first identity follows from
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}=\mathbb{S}_{0}
, cf. (
4.18
). The stated inequality follows by using Lemma
4.2
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}C_{k}^{\varepsilon}\|{\varphi}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})}^{2},\,C_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\|{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon})}(c^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{-2}\|1_{k,k}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}^{2},

and we conclude like in the proof of Proposition
4.5
.
∎

A remark on notation

Sometimes we will need to apply
\mathbb{P}_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\perp}
etc. to forms or functions defined on a single boundary
\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. The distinction between the projectors on spaces on
\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
and on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
is not reflected in the notation.

4.3
Density error

The goal of this section is the error analysis for the scattered field, namely the proof of Theorem
4.2
. The well-posedness result of Theorem
4.1
will be a by-product of the construction.
The starting point of the proof of Theorem
4.2
is obtaining an appropriate bound on the error of the density, which will be further translated into the error of the scattered field, by applying the single-layer potential to this error, cf. (
4.6
) and (
4.1
).

We study the density error
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}
, namely the difference between the exact density
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}
satisfying (
4.2
) and approximate density
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, see (
4.3
), i.e.

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}.

Our first goal is to obtain a bound on the density error
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}
that is explicit in terms of
\varepsilon
,
\omega
,
N
and
d_{*}^{\varepsilon}
. From now on we fix the frequency
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
.

We remark that
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the following SL BIE for the Helmholtz problem

\displaystyle S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G},\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}:=S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}.

(4.21)

At this point, one would be tempted to write

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}=\left(S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}),

(4.22)

and next use the usual operator-norm bound

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}\|\leq\|(S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\|\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}\|.

It can be shown, cf.
[
19
]
, that this strategy would lead to non-optimal error estimates. Instead, we proceed as follows. We split the error into two components, using the projectors
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
, defined in Section
4.2
:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}+\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}\in H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),

(4.23)

and estimate these two components separately. We will show that they have a different scaling with respect to
\varepsilon
, which has an incidence on the upper bound of the error between the approximate and the exact scattered fields. The choice of the above decomposition for the error is discussed in more detail in Section
4.4.2
, see Remark
9
.

Decomposition of (
4.21
).

Using the properties of the orthogonal projectors
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
and their adjoints
\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}
,
\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}
, see (
4.18
), we introduce the following notations for the restrictions of
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}
to the spaces
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
and
H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}}:\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}|_{H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}:H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp\sigma}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}|_{\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}}:\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G})^{a},

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp\perp}:=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}|_{H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}:H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G})^{a}.

(4.24)

We equip the corresponding domain spaces with the
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
-norm, while the range spaces with the
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
-norm.

Remark 7
.

Using (
4.3
), in particular, the asymptotic Galerkin model rewrites:

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}.

(4.25)

To see this, we recall the definition of the asymptotic Galerkin method:

\displaystyle\text{ find }\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\in\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon},\text{ s.t. }\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\overline{\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}.

In particular, since
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\left\{\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\right\}
, taking in the above
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\psi}
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
yields

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=\langle\boldsymbol{\psi},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}}\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},\quad\boldsymbol{\forall}\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),

and as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\in\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
, (
4.25
) follows.

Applying the splitting (
4.23
) and the notation (
4.3
) to (
4.21
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=0,

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}+\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}),

(4.27)

where in the first equation we used that
\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})=0
. Indeed,

\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon})=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-S^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}=0,

as follows from (
4.25
).

Remark 8
.

The projectors
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
are chosen so that the matrix in (
4.26
), (
4.27
) becomes close to diagonal, see the estimates of Theorem
4.3
.

An expression for
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}
.
From (
4.26
) we have that

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}=-(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}.

Thus, obtaining an upper bound on
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}
amounts to bounding the operators involved in the above expression, as well as finding an upper bound for
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
. To obtain this latter bound, it would be natural to use (
4.27
); however, a more efficient way is to follow the path of
[
19
]
, which allows to have better final bounds in terms of
\omega
.

An expression of
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
.

To obtain a close to optimal bound for the component orthogonal to the constants
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
, we recall the original identity (
4.22
),

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}=({S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}),

and next apply the orthogonal projector
\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}
to the both sides of the above identity:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}(\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})+\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}),

where we used again the Galerkin orthogonality to argue that
\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})=0
.

A strategy.
We thus end up with the two identities:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}=-(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}),

and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\|\|\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}\|\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},\\
&\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\|\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.\end{split}

(4.28)

Therefore, we see that estimating the error components in terms of
\varepsilon
amounts to obtaining estimates on the operator norms and the data
\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{G}^{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}
.

Bounds on operator norms and data.

The following theorem summarizes the upper bounds on the norms of the operators that appear in (
4.28
).

Theorem 4.3

(Estimates on operator norms)
.

There exists
C>0
, which depends only on
\Gamma_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\|(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\times C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3}),

(4.29)

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\|

\displaystyle\leq C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3}),

(4.30)
