\displaystyle\|\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp\sigma}\|,\;\|\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\perp}\|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{5/2}\times CN(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2},

(4.31)

where
\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*}=\min(1,d^{\varepsilon}_{*}).

Proof.

The stability bounds (
4.29
) and (
4.30
) are proven in Section
4.4.1
, while the continuity bound (
4.31
) is proven in Section
4.4.2
.
∎

Let us now present an auxiliary bound on the data, which will be used in the sequel. We first derive an intermediate result in the frequency domain, which will then be improved after obtaining the corresponding bounds in the time domain.

Theorem 4.4

(Bound on the data)
.

There exists
C>0
, which depends only on
\Gamma_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, the following bound holds true:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{\omega,N,d^{\varepsilon}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{3/2}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}),

with
C_{\omega,N,d^{\varepsilon}_{*}}:=CN(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-4}(1+|\omega|)^{4}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})
.

Proof.

See Section
4.4.3
.
∎

Inserting the bounds of Theorems
4.3
and
4.4
into (
4.28
) yields the following bounds:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{3/2}CN^{2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-10}(1+|\omega|)^{10}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-9})|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{3/2},

(4.32)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq CN(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-6}(1+|\omega|)^{6}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-6})|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{3/2},

where we used a temporary notation
|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{\alpha}:=\varepsilon^{\alpha}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}
. The following result makes the bounds (
4.32
) fully explicit in
\varepsilon>0
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\in H^{3}(B)
. Then there exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,
|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{3/2}\leq C\varepsilon^{3/2}\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{3}(B)}
.

Proof.

See Section
4.4.4
.
∎

Thus, our goal in this section is to prove the following bounds:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{3}\times CN^{2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-10}(1+|\omega|)^{10}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-9})\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{3}(B)},

(4.33)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{3/2}\times CN(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-6}(1+|\omega|)^{6}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-6})\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{3}(B)}.

We see that
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}
have a different scaling with respect to
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. It is this property that will allow to obtain an optimal error bound for the scattered field, see the discussion before (
4.22
).

4.4
Proofs of the technical results in Section
4.3

4.4.1
Proof of the stability bound on the operator norms
\|(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\|
,
\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\|

In this section, we prove stability bounds (
4.29
) and (
4.30
). Our strategy follows the strategy of similar estimates for circles in
[
19
]
, and relies on deriving a coercivity bound for the single layer boundary integral operator
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}
with an explicit coercivity constant. To do so, we follow a classical approach based on the lifting lemma adapted to the many-particle case (an idea due to
[
17
]
), which yields bounds explicit in
\varepsilon
. Let us define the following energy norm, for
\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
:

\displaystyle\|{v}\|_{a,\mathbb{R}^{3}}^{2}:=a^{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}.

(4.34)

For
V\in\{\mathbb{S}_{0},\,H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\}
we define the quantity (well-defined by the same argument as in proof of Proposition 2.5.1 in
[
28
]
)

\displaystyle L_{V}^{\varepsilon}(a):=\sup\limits_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in V\setminus\{0\}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\\
\gamma_{0}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{a,\mathbb{R}^{3}}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}\geq 0,

(4.35)

The proof of the result below resembles the proof of Proposition 4.4 in
[
19
]
. See Appendix
C
for details.

Proposition 4.10
.

There exists
C>0
, s.t. for all
a>0
,
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,

\displaystyle L_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\varepsilon}(a)\leq C_{a}\varepsilon^{-1/2},\quad L_{H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{\varepsilon}(a)\leq C_{a},

where
C_{a}=C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1}(1+a)^{1/2}\max(1,a^{-1})
.

This result enables us to prove the proposition below.

Proposition 4.11
.

For all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, the operator
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}:

H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow

H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})

is continuous and invertible. Moreover, for all
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Im}\langle{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\eta}}},\overline{\omega S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{2}(1+|\omega|)^{-1}\min(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{3})

\displaystyle\times\left(\varepsilon\|\mathbb{P}^{*}_{0}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\mathbb{P}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\right).

Proof.

Step 1. Proof of coercivity estimate.

Let us define
v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
We will use the following identity that follows from the Green’s formula and the definition of the layer potentials: for any
\Lambda\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, s.t.
\gamma_{0}\Lambda=\boldsymbol{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\Delta v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\Lambda\,d\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\nabla v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\nabla\Lambda d\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=-\omega^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\Lambda d\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\nabla v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\nabla\Lambda d\boldsymbol{x}=:a_{\omega}(v^{\varepsilon}_{\eta},{\Lambda}),\end{split}

(4.36)

see the proof of
[
28
, Proposition 2.6.1, after (2.17)]
. The above with
{\Lambda}=\overline{\omega\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}}
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\operatorname{Im}\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{\omega S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle&=-\operatorname{Im}\left(\overline{\omega}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}-|\omega|^{2}\omega\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\right)\\
&=\operatorname{Im}\omega\|v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|^{2}_{|\omega|,\mathbb{R}^{3}}.\end{split}

(4.37)

It remains to relate the norms of
v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
. With Proposition
4.2
,
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mathbb{P}^{*}_{0}\boldsymbol{\eta}+\mathbb{P}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\eta}
and we estimate these quantities separately. With Proposition
4.1
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}|}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{S}_{0}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}|}{\|\mathbb{P}_{0}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}},

and, using (
4.36
) and next the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{S}_{0}\setminus\{0\}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\\
\gamma_{0}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\end{subarray}}\frac{|a_{\omega}(v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\Lambda^{\varepsilon})|}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}

\displaystyle\leq\|v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{|\omega|,\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\mathbb{S}_{0}\setminus\{0\}}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\\
\gamma_{0}\Lambda^{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{|\omega|,\mathbb{R}^{3}}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}=\|v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{|\omega|,\mathbb{R}^{3}}L^{\varepsilon}_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}(|\omega|),

(4.38)

see (
4.35
). Proceeding in a similar manner for
\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|v^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{|\omega|,\mathbb{R}^{3}}L_{H^{1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{\varepsilon}(|\omega|).

(4.39)

Summing up the bounds ((
4.38
)), ((
4.39
)), using the result in (
4.37
), and next employing Proposition
4.10
yields

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Im}\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{\omega S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle\gtrsim(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{2}(1+|\omega|)^{-1}\min(1,|\omega|^{2})\operatorname{Im}\omega(\varepsilon\|\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}+\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}).

The stated coercivity estimate is obtained with
\min(1,|\omega|^{2})\operatorname{Im}\omega\gtrsim\min(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{3})
.

Step 2. Proof that
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}
is continuous and invertible.

Once we argue that
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}:\,H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
is continuous and coercive, the Lax-Milgram lemma, cf.
[
24
, Lemma 2.32]
implies that it is invertible.
The continuity and coercivity of the single layer boundary integral operator were established in
[
4
]
for a single obstacle, based on the energy-type argument easily extendable to the multiple-obstacle case. Since an optimal continuity estimate is not important in what follows, we omit the proof.
∎

We will use the coercivity bound of Proposition
4.11
in a modified form, see below.

Theorem 4.5
.

For all
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Im}\langle{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\eta}}},e^{-i\operatorname{Arg}(\omega)}\overline{S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\rangle

\displaystyle\gtrsim c_{S}\left(\varepsilon\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\right),

\displaystyle c_{S}

\displaystyle=(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{2}(1+|\omega|)^{-2}\min(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{3}).

Proof.

The result is immediate by combining Proposition
4.11
, Proposition
4.6
and the bound
|\omega|^{-1}\geq(1+|\omega|)^{-1}
.
∎

The above results yield a stability bound for the asymptotic Galerkin method (
4.29
).

Corollary 4.1
.

For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, the operator
\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}:\,\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{0}
is invertible, and satisfies

\displaystyle\|(\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma})^{-1}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\,\omega)^{-3}).

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}=\left.\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\right|_{\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}}:\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\to\mathbb{S}_{0}
. Since
\dim(\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G})=\dim(\mathbb{S}_{0})<+\infty
, it is sufficient to show that
a(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\,\boldsymbol{\eta}):=\langle\boldsymbol{\varphi},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}
satisfies an
\operatorname{inf}
-
\operatorname{sup}
-condition on
\mathbb{S}_{0}\times\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
, see
[
8
, Section 3.4.2]
. In particular, for
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, we write

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2},

(4.40)

where we used (
4.17
)(b) and
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}
. By Theorem
4.5
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathbb{P}_{0}^{*}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}\right|\gtrsim c_{s}\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},

which implies that
\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}
is invertible. The stability bound follows by remarking that
|\langle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma},\overline{\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma\sigma}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2,\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}|
satisfies the above coercivity bound, see (
4.40
).
∎

Similarly, we obtain the stability bound (
4.30
).

Corollary 4.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
the following bound holds true:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}\leq C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\,\omega)^{-3}).

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, then, by Proposition
4.11
, there exists
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
such that
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.
By Theorem
4.5
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C^{-1}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}\right|.

(4.41)

With
S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, we replace in the above

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\langle\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}=\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\eta},\overline{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2},

which implies, by recalling that
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, the desired bound

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C^{-1}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})\|\boldsymbol{\varphi}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

∎

4.4.2
Proof of the continuity bound on the operator norms (
4.31
)

In this section, we obtain a bound on the operator norm of the ’off-diagonal’ terms (
4.31
). We start with the operator
\mathbb{S}_{\sigma\perp}^{\varepsilon}
. First, remark that

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\sigma\perp}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\left.S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\right|_{H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}:\,H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{0}

satisfies: for all
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
,
\mathbb{S}_{\sigma\perp}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varphi},

see (
4.17
)(a). Therefore,

\|\mathbb{S}_{\sigma\perp}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}\leq\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))},

and we will estimate this latter norm, recalling the following identity:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\sigma}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\lambda},\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle_{1/2,-1/2}|}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}.

(4.42)

In what follows, let us set
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\perp}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}
.
The duality product in the above can be split into two terms, which correspond to self- and cross-interactions:

\displaystyle\langle S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\lambda},\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle_{1/2,-1/2}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}}_{C_{si}}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{\ell\neq k}\langle S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,\ell},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}}_{C_{ci}}.

(4.43)

Recall that
S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}
is defined in (
4.5
). The above terms
C_{si}
and
C_{ci}
will be handled in different manners.

Estimating self-interacting terms
C_{si}

By density, it suffices to consider
C_{si}
only for functions
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. In this case, the duality product writes

\displaystyle\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{1/2,-1/2}=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|){\lambda}_{\perp,k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\;v_{\sigma,k}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}.

(4.44)

Let us now introduce the following new integral kernel (stems from the Taylor expansion of
\mathrm{e}^{i\omega r}
around
\omega=0
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(r)

\displaystyle:=G_{\omega}(r)-G_{0}(r)-\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}=\frac{e^{i\omega r}-1-i\omega r}{4\pi r},\quad r>0,

and the associated operator in
\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k}\varphi=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(4.45)

\displaystyle E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}=\gamma_{0}\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k},\quad\text{so that}\quad E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}.

With this new notation, we can rewrite

\displaystyle(S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=(S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}+(E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k},

and inserting the above expression with
\varphi=\lambda_{\perp,k}
into (
4.44
) yields

\displaystyle\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle+\frac{i\omega}{4\pi}\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\lambda_{\perp,k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\langle 1,v_{\sigma,k}\rangle+\langle E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}\mathbb{P}_{\perp}^{*}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle.

In the above we used that
\langle S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle=\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},S_{0}^{\varepsilon,kk}v_{\sigma,k}\rangle=\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},1\rangle=0
(recall that
\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
) and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\perp}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. Next, we rewrite the above by using the symmetry of the integral kernel of
E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}
and the density argument:

\displaystyle\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{1/2,-1/2}=\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{-1/2,1/2}.

(4.46)

It remains thus to find a suitable estimate on the operator norm
E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}
. While right away we make use of
\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}
, for future computations we will need an estimate on the operator norm of
E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}
as well.

Proposition 4.12
.

There exists
C>0
which depends only on
\Gamma_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,N
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
and
k=1,\ldots,N
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}))}\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}|\omega|^{2},

(4.47)

\displaystyle\|E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}),L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}))}\leq C\varepsilon^{3}|\omega|^{2}.

(4.48)

The proof of this proposition is based on the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 4.3
.

The function
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Moreover, there exist
C_{k},C_{k}^{\prime}>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, it holds

\displaystyle(a)~\max_{\boldsymbol{x},\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|\leq C_{k}\varepsilon|\omega|^{2},\quad(b)~\max_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|\leq C_{k}^{\prime}|\omega|^{2}.

(4.49)

Proof.

From the definition of
r\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(r)
it is immediate that it is
C^{\infty}(0,\infty)
, therefore, in our case, it is sufficient to check that
r\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\omega}^{\prime}(r)
admits a limit as
r\rightarrow 0
. The desired result is obtained by a direct computation for

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\omega}^{\prime}(r)=\omega^{2}\frac{i\omega r\mathrm{e}^{i\omega r}-\mathrm{e}^{i\omega r}+1}{4\pi\omega^{2}r^{2}}.

(4.50)

Proof of (
4.49
)(a).

By the mean-value theorem, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{x},\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|\leq\max_{r\in[0,\operatorname{diam}\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}]}|\mathcal{K}_{\omega}^{\prime}(r)|\operatorname{diam}\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon},

(4.51)

where we used
\max_{\boldsymbol{x},\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|=\max_{r\in[0,\operatorname{diam}\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}]}|\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(r)|
and
\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(0)=0
.
Next, a direct computation (
4.50
) shows that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\omega}=\omega^{2}f(\omega r)
,
with
f(z)=\frac{z\mathrm{e}^{iz}-\mathrm{e}^{iz}+1}{4\pi z^{2}}
. The function
z\mapsto f(z)
is analytic in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, continuous up to the boundary of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
(i.e. well-defined on
\mathbb{R}
), is uniformly bounded by
C/|z|
,
C>0
, for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}
s.t.
|z|\rightarrow+\infty
. By the maximum principle,
z\mapsto|f(z)|
is bounded by a constant. Thus,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\omega}(r)|\leq C|\omega|^{2},\quad\forall\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+},\quad r\geq 0.

(4.52)

With (
4.51
), this yields the desired bound.

Proof of (
4.49
)(b).
We start by remarking that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})}{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}.

(4.53)

Using (
4.52
) yields the desired bound (
4.49
)(b).

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.12
.

Proof of (
4.47
).

Let
\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
. We use the norm equivalence result of Proposition
4.1
:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\lesssim|E^{\varepsilon,{kk}}_{\omega}\varphi|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

(4.54)

To estimate the
H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
-seminorm, we make use of Lemma
A.2
. To justify its use, let us remark that for
\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
,
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,k}_{\omega}\varphi\in C^{1}(\overline{B^{\varepsilon}_{k}})
, cf. Lemma
4.3
:

\displaystyle|E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\varphi|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{3/2}\|\nabla\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,k}_{\omega}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

(4.55)

Using (
4.52
)(b) allows us to interchange
\nabla
and integration, and we obtain

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,k}_{\omega}\varphi)(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathcal{K}_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)\varphi(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\Rightarrow\|\nabla\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon,k}_{\omega}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k}\varepsilon|\omega|^{2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})},

where the last bound follows from (
4.49
)(b), the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the bound
\|1\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\lesssim\varepsilon
. Finally, combining the above bound with (
4.55
) yields

\displaystyle|E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\varphi|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\leq C_{k}\varepsilon^{5/2}|\omega|^{2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})},

which, with (
4.54
) results in the desired statement.

Proof of (
4.48
).
Let
\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})
. We make use of Lemma
A.1
which gives

\displaystyle\|E_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,kk}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\lesssim\varepsilon\|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

We proceed like in the previous step but use now (
4.52
)(a).
∎

Now we can plug the bound of Proposition
4.12
into the identity (
4.46
). Applying Proposition
4.4
, in order to control the
L^{2}
norm of
v_{\sigma,k}
via the
H^{-1/2}
norm, we obtain

\displaystyle|\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|

\displaystyle=|\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E^{\varepsilon,k}_{\omega}v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}E^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|\lambda_{\perp,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|v_{\sigma,k}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\varepsilon^{5/2}|\omega|^{2}\|\lambda_{\perp,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|v_{\sigma,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

Combining this bound with the definition of
C_{si}
in (
4.43
) and using the
\ell_{2}
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields the following key bound:

\displaystyle C_{si}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}|\omega|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

(4.56)

Estimating cross-interacting terms
C_{ci}

We start with one of the terms, for
\ell\neq k
. By the symmetry of the integral kernel and density argumentm

\displaystyle\langle S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,\ell k}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,\ell}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}=\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}=\langle\lambda_{\perp,k},\mathbb{P}_{\perp}S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}.

Applying the norm equivalence of Proposition
4.1
yields

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbb{P}_{\perp}S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell},\lambda_{\perp,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|\lesssim|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|\lambda_{\perp,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

(4.57)

Since
|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}v|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}=|v-\mathbb{P}_{0}v|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}=|v|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}
, we also have that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbb{P}_{\perp}S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell},\lambda_{\perp,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}|\lesssim|S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\|\lambda_{\perp,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

Finally, with Lemma
A.2
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}v_{\sigma,\ell}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}&\leq C\varepsilon^{3/2}\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)v_{\sigma,\ell}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{k}^{\varepsilon})}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}\max_{\boldsymbol{x}\in B_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{y}\in B_{k}^{\varepsilon}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|\|v_{\sigma,{\ell}}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})},\end{split}

(4.58)

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
\boldsymbol{x}\rightarrow G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)
is a
C^{\infty}
function, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the bound
\|1\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})}\lesssim\varepsilon
. Since

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)=\frac{i\omega\mathrm{e}^{i\omega|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})}{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{i\omega|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{3}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}),

it is straightforward to see that for
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)|\lesssim\max(1,|\omega|)|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{-1}\left(1+|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|^{-1}\right)\lesssim(1+|\omega|)(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}.

(4.59)

Combining the above and (
4.58
) into (
4.57
), we conclude that

\displaystyle|\langle S_{\omega}^{\varepsilon,k\ell}\lambda_{\perp,\ell},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{5/2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}\max(1,|\omega|)\|v_{\sigma,\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})}\|\lambda_{\perp,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}.

The above, Proposition
4.4
, (
4.43
) and the
\ell_{2}
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yield:

\displaystyle C_{ci}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}\max(1,|\omega|)(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\|\lambda_{\perp,\ell}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|v_{\sigma,k}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}N\max(1,|\omega|)(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

(4.60)

Proof of the bound (
4.31
)

We combine the bounds (
4.56
) and (
4.60
) into (
4.4.2
):

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\mathbb{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{L}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{5/2}N(1+|\omega|)^{2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}\times

\displaystyle\times\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}},

and the desired bound is immediate from Proposition
4.5
.

The bound for
\mathbb{S}_{\perp\sigma}^{\varepsilon}
can be obtained similarly, and thus we omit its proof.

Remark 9
.

From the above proof, it follows that that the behaviour of the self-interacting terms
\langle S^{\varepsilon,kk}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,k},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}
is very sensitive to the proper choice of the space decomposition of the solution space
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
; at the same time, it can be shown that for the cross-interacting terms
\langle S^{\varepsilon,k\ell}_{\omega}\lambda_{\perp,\ell},v_{\sigma,k}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}
, replacing
v_{\sigma,k}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
by elements of
\mathbb{S}_{0}
would still yield an estimate of order
\varepsilon^{5/2}
.

4.4.3
Proof of Theorem
4.4

The goal of this section is to prove that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\lesssim C_{\omega,N,d^{\varepsilon}_{*}}(\varepsilon^{3/2}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}),

with
C_{\omega,N,d^{\varepsilon}_{*}}:=N(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-4}(1+|\omega|)^{4}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})
.
The proof relies on the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 4.4

(A stability bound on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, the Galerkin density
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, defined in (
4.3
), satisfies

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\times C(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-2}(1+|\omega|)^{2}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-3})\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

Proof.

From (
4.25
) it follows that
\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|\left(\mathbb{S}_{\sigma\sigma}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}\|\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}^{*}\boldsymbol{\hat{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}
. It remains to combine Corollary
4.1
and Proposition
4.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

We begin by applying the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G})\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

By Proposition
4.7
, for the first term in the rhs of the above, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\lesssim|\hat{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{g}}}^{\varepsilon}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

To estimate the second term, we use that
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}={S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, see (
4.21
), and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{S}_{\perp\sigma}^{\varepsilon}\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}\implies\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\|\mathbb{S}_{\perp\sigma}^{\varepsilon}\|\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{G}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

The desired inequality then follows by combining the bound (
4.31
) and Lemma
4.4
.
∎

4.4.4
Proof of Proposition
4.9

Since
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\in H^{3}(B)
, by the Sobolev embedding
[
3
, Theorem 4.12, part 2]
,
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B})
,
\alpha<1/2
. By Lemma
A.1
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(B^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon N\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(B)}.

By Proposition
4.3
and Lemma
A.2
,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{P}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{\perp}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{3}\|\nabla\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{\varepsilon}_{k})}^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{3}\times C_{\perp}N\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|^{2}_{H^{3}(B)}.

4.5
Proof of Theorem
4.2

Using the splitting of the density error (
4.23
), we introduce the field error in the Fourier-Laplace domain via the single layer potential operator, see (
4.1
),

\displaystyle\hat{e}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})-\hat{u}^{\varepsilon}_{\operatorname{app}}(\boldsymbol{x})=(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma})(\boldsymbol{x})+(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp})(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{\varepsilon,c}.

(4.61)

Now that we have bounds on
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
derived in (
4.33
), it remains to understand how the single-layer potential behaves on the corresponding subspaces.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
K\subset B^{c}
be a compact set. Then there exists a constant
C_{K}>0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}:\,H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Omega^{\varepsilon,c})
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow L^{\infty}(K)}\leq C_{K}N^{1/2}\varepsilon,

(4.62)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})\rightarrow L^{\infty}(K)}\leq C_{K}N^{1/2}\varepsilon^{3/2}(1+|\omega|).

(4.63)

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}\in K
. We fix
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
and next use the density argument. Since, in this case,
\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, and
|G_{\omega}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y})|\leq(4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)^{-1}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|),\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v})_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\right|\leq\|G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon N^{1/2}}{4\pi\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{x},\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},

where we used
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}\leq C_{k}\varepsilon\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}
, by Lemma
A.1
. Propositions
4.4
and
4.5
yield the desired estimate (
4.62
).

To prove (
4.63
), we proceed similarly. Since
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}=H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
, cf. (
4.16
), we have

\displaystyle\left|(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\langle\mathbb{P}_{\perp}G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|),\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|)|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},

cf. Proposition
4.3
.
With Lemma
A.2
, we have that

\displaystyle|G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|)|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3/2}\|\nabla G_{\omega}(|\boldsymbol{x}-\cdot|)\|_{L^{\infty}(B^{\varepsilon}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3/2}(1+|\omega|)\max(1,\operatorname{dist}(\boldsymbol{x},\Gamma^{\varepsilon})^{-2}),

where in the last inequality we made use of (
4.59
).
This yields the final bound.
∎

Combining Lemma
4.5
and (
4.33
) into (
4.61
) yields the desired bound:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\hat{{e}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x})|&\leq C_{K}\varepsilon^{3}N^{3/2}(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-6}\left(1+\varepsilon N(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-4}\right)(1+|\omega|)^{10}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-9})\\
&\times\|\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{3}(B)},\end{split}

(4.64)

where
C_{K}>0
depends on
K
and the shape of the scatterers
\Gamma_{j},\,j=1,\ldots,N
.

Remark 10
.

Splitting the error into two parts allows to prove a higher convergence order w.r.t.
\varepsilon
for the approximated scattered field, compared to the approach without the splitting. Indeed, using the worst of the two bounds for the error components yields
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}=O(\varepsilon^{3/2})
, which implies that
|(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon})(\boldsymbol{x})|=O(\varepsilon^{5/2})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the other hand, when applying the above splitting into two terms, we obtain an improved estimate of order
O(\varepsilon^{3})
.

Now we have necessary frequency-domain ingredients to prove the main statement of the paper for the model (
2.15
) in the time domain.

5
Proof of Theorem
2.1
for the model (
2.15
)

In the section that follows we aim at proving the following two results, which are refinement of the statement of Theorem
2.1
, for the model (
2.15
).

5.1
Well-posedness

\lambda_{G,k}^{\varepsilon}

\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}_{G}

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}.

Theorem 5.1

(Well-posedness of (
2.15
))
.

Assume that
u^{\operatorname{inc}}\in TD(H^{3}(B))\cap C^{3}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};H^{3}(B))
. Then the asymptotic model (
2.15
) admits a unique solution in
L^{\prime}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
. Moreover, the coefficients
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\in\operatorname{TD}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap C(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};\mathbb{R}^{N})
and satisfy the following stability bound:

\displaystyle\|\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{N})}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\times C(T,N,(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1})\|u^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{3}(0,T;H^{3}(B))},\quad\text{ for all }T>0,

where
C=C(T,N,(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1})
depends polynomially on
N
,
(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1}
,
T
.

Proof.

Remark that
\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{TD}(H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))\cap C^{3}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
, by assumption on
u^{\operatorname{inc}}
. To prove the existence and uniqueness, we apply the Fourier-Laplace transform to (
2.15
), which yields (
4.5
).
By Theorem
4.1
, the problem (
4.5
) admits a unique solution for all
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. By assumption on
u^{\operatorname{inc}}
,
\omega\mapsto\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Moreover, the mapping
\omega\mapsto(S^{\varepsilon}_{\omega})^{-1}
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, cf.
[
15
, Lemma 13, p. 592]
. Therefore, the density
\omega\mapsto\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}(\omega)
is analytic on
\mathbb{C}^{+}
.

Moreover, since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies polynomial stability bounds in the Laplace domain, cf. Theorem
4.1
(because
\hat{u}^{\operatorname{inc}}
satisfies them as well), it is a Fourier-Laplace transform of a distribution in
\operatorname{TD}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}))
. See Section
2.2.2
for more details.

Since
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}=\left(\mathcal{I}^{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
, the desired result follows for the coefficients of expansion of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
, cf. (
5.1
).

To prove the time-domain stability result and a more precise regularity on
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
(and thus on
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\varepsilon}_{G}
), we invoke the Plancherel identity combined with the causality argument. A complete proof can be found in
[
19
, Section 4.5.2]
or
[
28
, Proof of Proposition 3.2.2]
.
Note that the Plancherel identity provides an
L^{2}
bound on the scattered field in the time domain. To obtain a bound in the
L^{\infty}
norm, we use the fact that for an
X
-valued function
t\mapsto v(t)
, of a Banach space
X
, with
v(0)=0
, it holds true that
\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;X)}\leq T^{1/2}\|\partial_{t}v\|_{L^{2}(0,T;X)}
. This implies that, in the final estimate, the data must possess one additional order of time regularity; in this case the solution belongs to
C([0,T];X)
for all
T>0
, because it is
H^{1}(0,T;X)
by the Plancherel identity, and because of the embedding
H^{1}(0,T;X)
into
C([0,T];X)
. ∎

5.2
Convergence

Theorem 5.2

(Pointwise estimate on the field error)
.

Let
K\subset B^{c}
be a compact set, and
u^{\operatorname{inc}}\in C^{11}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0};H^{3}(B))
. Then, for all
T>0
,

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}-u^{\varepsilon}_{G,\operatorname{\operatorname{app}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(K))}

\displaystyle\leq C(T,N,(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1})\varepsilon^{3}\|u^{\operatorname{inc}}\|_{H^{11}(0,T;H^{3}(B))},

where
C=C(T,N,(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1})
depends polynomially on
N
,
(\underline{d}^{\varepsilon}_{*})^{-1}
,
T
.

Proof.

Follows by combining (
4.64
) together with the Plancherel identity and the causality argument, a complete proof can be bound in
[
19
, Section 4.5.2]
or
[
28
, Proof of Proposition 3.2.2]
.
∎

6
Derivation and analysis of the simplified model (
2.17
)

6.1
Motivation

While the model (
2.15
) provides an approximation of the far-field with an absolute error of
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, its numerical realization appears to be quite involved. It can be done with the help of e.g. the boundary element method in space combined with an appropriate discretization scheme in time, e.g. Convolution Quadrature method
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
. In this context, such a numerical realization relies on

•

meshing the domains
\Omega^{\varepsilon}
and defining an appropriate (’simple’) boundary element basis with functions supported on a few elements of the meshes;

•

computation of the densities
\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}
expressed in such a basis;

•

evaluation of the double surface integrals in
K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}
;

•

evaluation of the single surface integrals occurring in the expressions for the scattered field and in the right-hand side of the system (
3.8
).

The obvious bottleneck in the above is the evaluation of the entries of the double surface integrals in
K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}
. The goal of this section is to suggest an alternative model, which does not rely on such computations.

6.2
Derivation and analysis of the model (
2.17
)

To derive the model under consideration, let us re-state it and explain the intuition behind it. Given

\displaystyle c_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},\quad\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}:=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{y}\sigma_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}c_{k}^{\varepsilon},

(6.1)

we approximate the scattered field by

\displaystyle\begin{split}{u}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\operatorname{app}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)&=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|}\left(c_{k}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{3}}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}\right)\\
&+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\partial_{t}\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon},\end{split}

(6.2)

where the time-dependent functions
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k}:\,\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
solve

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}(K^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}*\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{s,k})(t)=-u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)c_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon},\text{ with }

(6.3)

\displaystyle K^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k})(t).

and the matrix
\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s}
is given by, with
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}:=c^{\varepsilon}_{k}/4\pi
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}(\omega)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}4\pi i\omega(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{2}j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})h^{(1)}_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}),&k=\ell,\\
4\pi i\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k}\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{k})j_{0}(\omega\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})h^{(1)}_{0}(\omega|\boldsymbol{c}_{k}-\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}|),&k\neq\ell.\end{array}\right.

To explain the idea behind the above, we resort to
[
12
]
. As argued in this paper, to obtain the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
-accurate model in the far-field, it is sufficient to know capacitance of the obstacles only. In other words, the fields scattered by Lipschitz obstacles and by the spheres of equivalent capacitance would be almost the same.

The first idea is thus the following: one could replace obstacles by balls of equivalent capacitance, and use the asymptotic Galerkin model (
2.15
) for balls, with the basis functions being now obstacle-wise constant (since
\sigma_{j}^{\varepsilon}=1
). Importantly, the corresponding model will preserve its stability properties, since it is still the Galerkin discretization of the single-layer boundary integral operator, see
[
5
]
. Moreover, in this case all the Galerkin integrals are known analytically, cf. e.g.
[
19
]
for the circles. Later on, we will argue that this procedure indeed yields (
6.3
), see Proposition
6.2
.

However, this may potentially lead to a loss of accuracy compared to (
2.15
): indeed, the model (
2.15
) yields
O(\varepsilon^{3})
error in the far-field, rather than
O(\varepsilon^{2})
obtained in the frequency-domain
[
12
]
. As we will see later, this loss of accuracy can be avoided by adding more terms in the approximation of the far-field and in the data, which requires a rather minor effort from the computational viewpoint.

6.2.1
Derivation of the simplified model (
2.17
) from (
2.15
)

The model (
2.17
) can be derived also starting with the model (
2.15
). To see this, we compare the two models, starting with simplest differences:

•

the data in the right-hand side of (
2.15
) is changed to

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rightarrow-\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}(u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)+\nabla u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}))\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}.

This replacement comes from the Taylor expansion of
u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
around
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}
.

•

the convolution operator in the left-hand side of (
2.15
) is approximated via (here we write the corresponding expression in a formal manner
4
4

4

These expressions should be understood as
\langle K_{G,\ell k},\varphi\rangle_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}}=\iint_{\Gamma_{\ell}\times\Gamma_{k}}\frac{\varphi(|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}
)

\displaystyle K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}(t)=\iint_{\Gamma_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\times\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow K^{\varepsilon}_{s,\ell k}(t)=4\pi(c^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}c^{\varepsilon}_{k})^{-1}\iint_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}\times\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\delta(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}}d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},

(6.4)

where
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
is a sphere centered in
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}
of a capacitance
c_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
. The last equality is a subject of Proposition
6.2
.

Remark that while
K_{G}
is nothing else but a semi-discretization of
{S}^{\varepsilon}
on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
with the ’asymptotic’ Galerkin space
\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
(see (
3.2
)),
K_{s}
is the semi-discretization of the single-layer
S^{\varepsilon}
on the spheres centered at
\{\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
, with capacitances
c_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
, with the asymptotic Galerkin space for the spheres (elements of
\mathbb{S}_{0}
).

•

finally, the convolution solution operator in (
2.15
), cf. (
2.13
), is replaced by

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\delta(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|}\left(c_{k}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\frac{d}{dt}\delta(t-|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|)}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k}|^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{k}^{\varepsilon}.

The above comes by writing (
2.13
) in the Fourier-Laplace domain, with the use of

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\varphi})(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{i\omega|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}\varphi_{k}(\boldsymbol{y})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{y}},

expanding
\boldsymbol{y}\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{e}^{i\omega|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}|}
around
\boldsymbol{c}_{k}
into the Taylor series, neglecting higher-order terms and going back to the time domain.

6.2.2
A roadmap of the analysis

Like in previous sections, we will derive our estimates in the frequency domain. We will concentrate our efforts on deriving an appropriate error estimate, while the corresponding well-posedness/stability estimate will appear as a by-product of the intermediate results, cf. e.g. Lemma
4.4
which proves Theorem
4.1
, that, in turn, implies by standard arguments Theorem
5.1
. As before, we will work with boundary densities, and next estimate the error of the scattered field. From the discussion in the previous paragraph, it may seem that this comparison of the boundary densities makes no sense, since, to derive the model, we replace obstacles by balls, and thus the densities are defined on different surfaces! Such a comparison will be done through the coefficients of the expansion of these densities into their respective bases. Recall that, for the ’standard’ Galerkin Foldy-Lax model (
2.15
), we define
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{G}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\lambda_{G,k}^{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x})
, see (
3.3
); in a similar manner, we define a new density, associated to the model (
6.3
), but living on
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{s}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},t):=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\lambda_{s,k}^{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\varepsilon}_{k}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon},\,t>0.

Let us introduce the quantities to estimate. As discussed in the previous section, we will take into account the effects of different approximations separately.
For this we introduce the following intermediate quantities. First of all, for
(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)}\in\operatorname{TD}(H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})),\quad\text{s.t.}\quad({S}^{\varepsilon}*\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)})(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon,(1)}(\boldsymbol{x},t),

(6.5)

\displaystyle g^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x},t):=-u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)-\nabla u^{\operatorname{inc}}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell},t)\cdot(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}),\,(\boldsymbol{x},t)\in\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\times\mathbb{R},

which is the solution to the original problem (
3.2
) with a perturbed right-hand side.
Next, we approximate (
6.5
) with the help of the asymptotic Galerkin method (
2.15
), which yields another density:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G}\in\operatorname{TD}(\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}(K^{\varepsilon}_{G,\ell k}*{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G,k})(t)=\int_{\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\sigma^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{x})d\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{x}},

(6.6)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\lambda^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G,k}(t)\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}).

Then the error between the exact density
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}
and the density
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{s}
will be measured by means of comparing them to the above newly introduced quantities. In other words, we define three error terms
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\varepsilon,(1)}
, which are simpler to estimate and whose sum allows to bound the sought error:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{s}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\varepsilon,(1)}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})},\\
&\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\varepsilon,(1)}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)},\quad\boldsymbol{e}_{2}^{\varepsilon,(1)}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G},\quad\boldsymbol{e}_{3}^{\varepsilon,(1)}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{G}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\varepsilon}_{s}.\end{split}

(6.7)

Let us remark that it is the error
\boldsymbol{e}_{3}^{\varepsilon,(1)}
that carries an information about the change in the operators
K
when passing from (
2.15
) to (
2.17
).

As soon as the density error is controlled, we will show how they allow us to bound the error in the scattered field, see Section
6.2.6
. As discussed in the beginning of this section, we will concentrate on the estimates in the frequency domain, which we further translate into the time domain.

Since most of our analysis relies on the existing estimates, in most of the arguments we will no longer keep track of exact powers of
|\omega|
and
\operatorname{Im}\omega
, but rather hide them in the notation, by remarking that the dependence is polynomial in
|\omega|
and inverse-polynomial in
\min(1,\operatorname{Im}\omega)
where needed. Same refers to the dependence on the quantities
d_{*}^{\varepsilon}
and
N
. More precisely, we will write that

\displaystyle a\leq C_{\operatorname{geom}}(N,\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})p(|\omega|,\operatorname{Im}\omega)b,

if there exists
m_{1},m_{2},n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0
and a constant
C>0
(independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
), s.t. for all
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\displaystyle a\leq CN^{n_{1}}(\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})^{-n_{2}}(1+|\omega|)^{m_{1}}\max(1,(\operatorname{Im}\omega)^{-m_{2}})b.

6.2.3
Error stemming from the simplification of the right-hand side
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}^{\varepsilon,(1)}

Remark that the error
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}^{\varepsilon,(1)}
is the error of the replacing the right-hand side in the original problem (
3.2
) by a simplified quantity, and thus

\displaystyle{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}^{\varepsilon,(1)}={S}^{\varepsilon}_{\omega}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\varepsilon,(1)})=\hat{{\boldsymbol{g}}}^{\varepsilon}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}.

(6.8)

To analyze the corresponding errors, we proceed similarly to how it was done in Section
4.3
, by decomposing the error into different components
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}=\mathbb{Q}_{\sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}=\mathbb{Q}_{\perp}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1}\in H^{-1/2}_{\perp}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})
. Both terms are bounded in a rather direct manner. Testing (
6.8
) with
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1}
and using the coercivity bound of Theorem
4.5
yields

\displaystyle c_{S}(\varepsilon\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})})\lesssim|\langle\,\hat{{\boldsymbol{g}}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\rangle|+|\langle\,{\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\rangle|.

Using Propositions
4.4
and
4.3
, we conclude that

\displaystyle c_{S}\left(\varepsilon\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle+|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

The inequality

|a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}|^{2}\leq(|a_{1}|^{2}/\varepsilon+|a_{2}|^{2})(\varepsilon|b_{1}|^{2}+|b_{2}|^{2})
yields

\displaystyle c_{S}(\varepsilon\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}

\displaystyle\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})})\lesssim(\varepsilon\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\|^{2}_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times(\varepsilon^{-1}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}^{2}+|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})})^{1/2},

so that, with the definition of
c_{S}
from Theorem
4.5
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\perp}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{\operatorname{geom}}(N,\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})p(|\omega|,\operatorname{Im}\omega)|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{-1/2},

(6.9)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}_{1,\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{-1/2}C_{\operatorname{geom}}(N,\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})p(|\omega|,\operatorname{Im}\omega)|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}-\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}|\!|\!|_{-1/2},

(6.10)

with

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\boldsymbol{\psi}|\!|\!|_{\alpha}:=\varepsilon^{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}+|\boldsymbol{\psi}|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}.

(6.11)

6.2.4
Galerkin Foldy-Lax error
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}^{\varepsilon,(1)}

For the error term
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}^{\varepsilon,(1)}
we apply the same analysis as in Section
4.3
. The only difference in the estimates arises from the replacement of
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon}
by
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}
. The inequality (
4.32
) rewrites, see (
6.11
):

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\perp}^{\varepsilon,(1)}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{\operatorname{geom},\perp,2}(N,\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})p_{\perp,2}(|\omega|,\operatorname{Im}\omega)|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}|\!|\!|_{3/2},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\sigma}^{\varepsilon,(1)}\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma^{\varepsilon})}\leq\varepsilon^{3/2}\times C_{\operatorname{geom},\sigma,2}(N,\underline{d}_{*}^{\varepsilon})p_{\sigma,2}(|\omega|,\operatorname{Im}\omega)|\!|\!|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\varepsilon,(1)}|\!|\!|_{3/2}.
